Now OCR each frame with tesseract. Let's write a Python script to run and save text. Could parallelize with xargs? Let's do Python with multiprocessing. Need ensure tesseract reads psm. We'll use psm 6. Let's do simple loop and progress.
[tool-fd0] exec_command <- OCR all sampled frames ($ cat > /app/ocr_frames.py <<'PY' import os, subprocess, glob, concurrent.futures, time indir='/app/frames2' outdir='/app/ocr2' os.makedirs(outdir, exist_ok=True) files=sorted(glob.glob(indir+'/f*.png')) print('files',len(files)) def do(f): base=os.path.basename(f).replace('.png','.txt') out=os.path.join(outdir, base) if os.path.exists(out) and os.path.getsize(out)>0: return base try: r=subprocess.run(['tesseract',f,'stdout','--psm','6'],capture_output=True,text=True,timeout=30) text=r.stdout except Exception as e: text='ERROR '+str(e) with open(out,'w') as fp: fp.write(text) return base t0=time.time() with concurrent.futures.ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=8) as ex: for i,_ in enumerate(ex.map(do,files),1): if i%25==0: print(i, f'{time.time()-t0:.1f}s', flush=True) print('done',time.time()-t0) PY python3 /app/ocr_frames.py)

[tool-0bd] exec_command <- List prompt lines per frame ($ python3 - <<'PY' import glob, os, re files=sorted(glob.glob('/app/ocr2/f*.txt')) for f in files: txt=open(f).read().splitlines() prompts=[l.strip() for l in txt if re.match(r'^\s*>',l) and l.strip()!='>'] # Also mention rest prompt-only line? bare=[l.strip() for l in txt if l.strip()=='>'] if prompts or bare: extra=[] if bare: extra.append('(bare>)') print(os.path.basename(f)[:-4], ' | '.join(prompts+extra)) PY)

Let's understand the game's input flow:
In Infocom games, if a command includes an ambiguous reference or insufficient information, the parser prompts for missing object with a question, and the player types just the noun phrase (without `>`). The game shows the question (without `>`), and the player's typed response appears usually with a `>` prompt? Let's look at OCR frames containing "What do you want to pump up?" to see how the player's response appears.

Approach to algorithmically extract commands from OCR frames:
1. For each frame, extract all lines.
2. Identify lines that begin with `>` and are "complete" (non-empty after stripping `>`, and not ending with a partial marker). But partial markers are just OCR showing something; e.g., `>op_` indicates incomplete. We can detect incomplete commands by:
   - They often end with `_` (cursor at end, OCR reads underscore as part? Actually underscore not part of command).
   - Or are prefixes of a later longer command.
3. We can iterate frames and maintain a "current command set" of the most recently seen commands on screen. When a new command appears in the set that wasn't in the previous frame, add it to sequence)Skip. But when a command scrolls off, it disappears; a repeated command later (like `n` after moving elsewhere) would again appear as new.Skip

We need distinguish between a command persisting across frames and a repeated command. We can track by screen position (y coordinate) from tesseract TSV. Since commands scroll upward over time, the same command's y position changes across frames. We could track line positions to associate command instances across frames and detect new commands. That's complex but possible.

Let's write a Python script that:
- Opens video with cv2.VideoCapture.
- Iterates frames.
- For each frame, computes a simple hash/diff of the bottom region (downsampled grayscale) compared to previous processed frame.
- If changed significantly, OCR the bottom crop via tesseract (subprocess).
- Accumulate OCR results with timestamps/frame numbers.
- Save to a log.

- Parse all frames, extract prompt lines.
- Clean them: lowercase, strip leading `>`, replace OCR errors using custom mappings.
- For each frame, keep list of "visible commands" (normalized).
- Identify new commands by comparing the visible set to previous frame, but considering that a "new" command appears at the bottom (largest y). We don't have y info from OCR text; but from OCR2 we lost bounding boxes. We could re-extract TSV from stored frames? But we have frames2 pngs. We could run tesseract TSV for each frame now (already OCR files have text only). Another OCR pass with TSV would cost same as before.

We might instead use the existing OCR text files and simply aggregate unique commands that appear, ordering by first appearance. Because each command, once typed, appears in most subsequent frames until scrolled off. The first appearance of the final complete command (not partial) should be near when it was typed. Partial commands appear before completion. Let's list first appearance frame for each distinct complete command in the OCR2 prompt lists. If a command appears, disappears, and reappears (like `w`), it might be a distinct later occurrence or the same old line. But for repeated commands (e.g., `n` multiple times), first appearance happens once; subsequent `n` appearances are ignored if we just take unique. So unique-by-first-appearance would miss repeat commands. That won't work.

We need count occurrences of each command as distinct moves. Since repeated commands like `n`, `w`, `s`, `e` occur many times, we need sequence order.

Thus we need better than first-appearance.

Let's leverage the "Moves:" counter in header lines. Every frame OCR includes a header like "Room Score: 44 Moves: 22". The counter tells how many moves have been made up to that point. As commands are entered, the header updates. We can identify the exact moves (commands) that cause counter increments by looking at the bottom prompt line in high-fps OCR. But to map sequence, high-fps is the way.

Let's do the high-fps bottom OCR. It's the most reliable. We'll write a Python script using OpenCV + tesseract to process video frames where the bottom area changes.

But OCR at high fps via subprocess each frame sequentially might be slow because OpenCV read + tesseract setup ~0.2-0.3s, 5700 frames => 20-30min. We can reduce by sampling at 10fps or by change detection.

Let's first implement change detection and OCR only changed frames. Let's test on a segment to estimate number of OCR calls.

We'll write a script that:
- Opens video, loops frames (30fps).
- For each frame, compute a hash of the bottom region (say resize to 160x40 grayscale, hash).
- Keep previous hash; if different, append frame index to list and save crop? But we need OCR later. Instead, OCR immediately in same loop and log.
- Use multiprocessing? Simpler sequential but we can use a queue.

Let's test on first 10 seconds to see how many changed frames and OCR times. But before that, note the entire screen might change due to flicker even when idle? We'll see.

Actually a simpler route: extract bottom crops at 10fps (every 3rd frame) and OCR all of them. 190s * 10fps = 1900 crops. OCR 1900 * 0.2 = 6.3 min. That's fine and simpler. At 10fps, even fast commands will be captured because after pressing Enter the command remains visible for at least a fraction of a second. Actually if a command is typed very fast (say 5 chars in 0.5s), the complete line will appear in frames spanning at least 0.5s => 5 frames at 10fps. Even if typing at 20 chars/s, a 5-char cmd = 0.25s => 2-3 frames. OCR at 10fps may catch at least the completed line before it scrolls. Also after Enter, output is printed and command line remains for a while. So 10fps should be sufficient.

Let's test by extracting one bottom crop per frame at 10fps using ffmpeg `-vf fps=10,crop=1280:250:0:470` to PNG images. But output images could be many. We can pipe directly to tesseract? ffmpeg can output raw video to pipe, and Python can read. Easier: use OpenCV to capture at 10fps and write crops to temp files.

Let's write a Python script:
- cv2.VideoCapture('/app/video.mp4')
- fps = cap.get(cv2.CAP_PROP_FPS) = 30.
- Iterate all frames; every 3rd frame (approximately 10fps), crop bottom region y=470:720, x=0:1280.
- Save crop to a BytesIO/PNG file in /tmp/crops/fXXXX.png
- Then OCR all crops using tesseract (maybe parallel).
But disk for 1900 * (small png ~10-30KB) = 40MB, fine.
- Then analyze OCR output.

Alternatively, compute frame indices every 3rd. Let's do it.

But be careful: OpenCV might not seek accurately with time. We'll just read sequentially and take frame when index % 3 == 0. We need maybe sample at 10fps (every 3rd frame). We'll also record timestamp = frame_idx / 30.

Let's extract crops.
[tool-fd6] exec_command <- Extract bottom crops at ~10fps ($ cat > /app/extract_bottom.py <<'PY' import cv2, os video='/app/video.mp4' outdir='/app/bottom10' os.makedirs(outdir, exist_ok=True) cap=cv2.VideoCapture(video) fps=cap.get(cv2.CAP_PROP_FPS) print('fps',fps) idx=0 count=0 while True: ret, frame=cap.read() if not ret: break if idx % 3 == 0: # crop bottom region: y 470..720 crop=frame[470:720, 0:1280] out=f'{outdir}/b{idx:05d}.png' cv2.imwrite(out, crop) count+=1 idx+=1 cap.release() print('saved',count,'frames') PY python3 /app/extract_bottom.py)
✓ [approved]
[tool-fd6] exec_command -> fps 30.0
[tool-fd6] exec_command -> saved 1901 frames
[penguin] output cap clamped hard: max_tokens 256000 -> 80229 (context_window 128000, estimated input 46747)
Now OCR 1901 crops. Could be ~6 min. Let's do with 8 threads.
[tool-066] exec_command <- OCR bottom crops ($ cat > /app/ocr_bottom.py <<'PY' import os, subprocess, glob, concurrent.futures, time indir='/app/bottom10' outdir='/app/bottom_ocr' os.makedirs(outdir, exist_ok=True) files=sorted(glob.glob(indir+'/b*.png')) print('files',len(files)) def do(f): base=os.path.basename(f).replace('.png','.txt') out=os.path.join(outdir, base) if os.path.exists(out) and os.path.getsize(out)>0: return base try: r=subprocess.run(['tesseract',f,'stdout','--psm','6'],capture_output=True,text=True,timeout=30) text=r.stdout except Exception as e: text='ERROR '+str(e) with open(out,'w') as fp: fp.write(text) return base t0=time.time() with concurrent.futures.ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=10) as ex: for i,_ in enumerate(ex.map(do,files),1): if i%100==0: print(i, f'{time.time()-t0:.1f}s', flush=True) print('done',time.time()-t0) PY python3 /app/ocr_bottom.py)
✓ [approved]

The output got truncated, but the bottom OCR at 10fps gives a rich view of partial typing. We need extract complete commands. This is a lot of data, but now we can reconstruct each command as the player types and presses Enter:

For each typed command, the sequence appears:
- partial frames with `>` + prefix
- then final complete command e.g. `>move rug`
- then a `[bare]` prompt or output.

We can detect the final command as the last full form before the prompt becomes bare or before the command scrolls away.

But we need handle sub-prompts: e.g., frames 01578-01593:
```
>pump
>boat
```
Here `>pump` (complete) then `>boat` (complete). We'll include both as inputs.

Let's build an automated extractor:
- Read bottom OCR files in frame order.
- For each frame, collect lines that start with `>` and have text after `>` (not bare).
- We care about "command events": whenever a new prompt command is entered)Skip, the last/current command line at the bottom changes.
- We can detect new command events by tracking the "current cursor/prompt line": the last visible `>` line in the crop. When the user starts typing, the last `>` line grows. When they press Enter, that line remains as a completed command FOR a while, then output appears and the next prompt (bare) appears below it? Actually after pressing Enter, the game prints output below, and the prompt `>` line remains above output; the new prompt might appear later as bare `>` at the bottom. So the "last visible `>`" after Enter might still be the old command until output pushes it up enough that the next prompt appears.

We can track the evolution of the bottom-most `>` line across frames. Each time a new prompt command is entered, a new `>` line appears at the bottom with cursor text. It starts as `>` or `>prefix`. As the user types, it grows. When the user presses Enter, the line is finalized)Skip; then the game prints output and eventually a new `>` prompt appears at bottom (bare). Then the user types the next command.

Thus, to extract commands, we can:
- Consider each frame's set of `>` prompt lines.
- Identify the bottommost (last in OCR output) `>` line.
- Track its content over time. When it changes from one complete command to a new command (i.e., after a bare prompt), record the old complete command.

But OCR often combines multiple prompt lines (old commands above) plus the current one. The bottommost `>` line in the OCR text is the current input (or sometimes the output prompt line). We can parse lines in order and take the last `>` line as current input.

Let's test this on a segment to see. e.g., frames 00222-00324 show `>n` then `>n` then `>u` (with `>n` persisting) etc. Let's examine.

Maybe easier: Use the fact that after each final command, the game produces output that scrolls the command line uptons. At the end, the next prompt `>` (bare) appears. So the commands themselves appear in the bottom OCR files as `>` lines. If we scan every 10fps file and note each time a NEW complete command line appears at the bottom that wasn't there in the previous frame and isn't just the previous command growing, we can record.

Let's implement a heuristic:
1. For each frame in order, get list of `>` lines (prompt commands), e.g., `['n','n','u']`.
2. Normalize prompt strings: strip leading `>`, strip trailing `_`, collapse whitespace, fix obvious OCR? We'll get dirty.
3. Determine "current input" = last prompt in list (since OCR lists lines top to bottom; last is bottom). But the list order in OCR text corresponds to top-to-bottom; the last line is the bottommost. Usually the bottommost `>` line is the current prompt (the one where user is typing or just entered), and older commands appear above. This should work.

Let's verify on some frames:
- f0106 bottom OCR shows `>get`, `>s`, ... wait full file at b01050 shows:
```
>e
Forest Path
>s
```
The last `>` line is `>s`, likely the current command just entered. Good.
So current input = last prompt line with text.

- When output is long, the current prompt line may be the bottommost. When bare prompt appears and user hasn't typed, there's no `>` line with text; current input is None.

Thus algorithm:
- Iterate frames in order.
- For each, extract all prompt command strings (text after `>`), taking the LAST one as current input candidate.
- If current input is not None and differs from previous frame's current input, then either:
   - It's a partial (prefix of eventual command) – should not record until complete.
   - Or it's a new full command – record.

We can record a command only when we see the same full text stable for some frames AND then it disappears/scrolled (or the next command starts). But partial typing will produce many variants. We can determine final command as the longest prefix that appears before transitioning to bare prompt or another command.

Better: For each input event, collect all observed frames' current prompt strings during the interval while the prompt stays active. The event begins when a non-bare prompt appears (user starts typing) and ends when:
- the prompt text becomes empty (bare) after pressing Enter and output, OR
- the prompt text changes to a completely different command (new typing after bare).

During the event, the typed text increases over time (possibly with corrections/backspace). The final command is the last non-bare text observed before the transition to bare or a new prompt. But OCR might read `>get bag_` etc. We need clean.

We can simply iterate all bottom frames and group by "prompt sessions" based on the bottommost prompt text:
- Maintain `session` = (start_frame, list_of_texts)
- For each frame:
  - cur = last prompt text or None
  - If cur is None (bare or no prompt): flush current session (record final text), set session=None.
  - Else if session is None: start new session with cur.
  - Else: append cur (or update latest text) to session.
- At the end, flush.

But there's a catch: between commands, the bottommost prompt may still be the old command line while output is being printed (since no new prompt yet). If the old command line remains bottommost during output, cur won't be None until a new bare prompt appears. During output, the old command line is still the last `>`; that's fine because session remains the same, we keep appending same text. Eventually when new prompt appears as bare, cur becomes None and we flush old command. Then new session starts for next command.

After pressing Enter, though, the command line might scroll up and the game prints output; a new prompt (bare) appears. There might be a brief moment where the old command line is still bottommost because no new prompt yet; cur remains old command. Eventually bare prompt appears -> None -> flush.

But OCR at 10fps may miss the bare prompt and instead see old command line for all frames until next command starts. Still, when next typing starts, cur switches from old command to prefix of new command (different text) -> we'd treat as a new session without flushing old. We need handle: if session non-None and cur changes to something completely unrelated (new command), then old command must be flushed before starting new session. But cur could also change because of partial typing within same session (e.g., `>g` -> `>ge` -> `>get`), which is the same session evolving. We can detect session continuation as long as new cur is a prefix-extension of previous cur? Actually in typing, cur grows: `g` -> `ge` -> `get`. If second command starts after bare, cur changes from `open` to `g` (shorter unrelated). We can treat a change where cur is not an extension/prefix of the previous full command as the start of a new session.

Let's implement:
- Keep `last_final` as the recorded complete command for current session.
- For each frame with cur:
  - If no active session:
      active=True, texts=[cur], last_complete=cur
  - else:
      # Check if cur continues current typing (is a prefix-extension of the previously seen full text? Actually last_complete may be full command from previous frames; cur could be partial of a new command. If cur is unrelated (not a prefix of last_complete and last_complete is not a prefix of cur), then flush previous (record last_complete as command) and start new session.
      if cur == last_complete or cur.startswith(last_complete) or last_complete.startswith(cur):
          # still same session or partial/full extension
          last_complete = max_extension(cur,last_complete)?? We want longest text seen; but if someone backspaces, we might see shorter. Use final at flush as the last cur before transition? Hmm.
      else:
          flush last_complete; start new session cur.
  - When cur is None (bare): flush last_complete if any; reset.

Now, the algorithm relies on OCR reading the correct bottommost prompt. Sometimes OCR may include multiple `>` lines with the current at bottom, and our `last` capture works.

Let's implement and see how many commands we get and compare to 262. During this, we must clean OCR errors to get proper commands. But first let's get raw sessions.

Let's write a script to parse bottom OCR files formatted by frame number (bXXXXX.txt), extract bottommost prompt text, and group sessions. We'll output final texts per session (raw). Then inspect.

Let's implement.
[tool-e27] exec_command <- Extract command sessions from bottom OCR ($ cat > /app/extract_sessions.py <<'PY' import glob, os, re files=sorted(glob.glob('/app/bottom_ocr/b*.txt')) def last_prompt(lines): prompts=[] for l in lines: s=l.strip() if s.startswith('>') and s != '>': txt=s[1:].strip() # strip trailing underscore artifact often means cursor prompts.append(txt) if not prompts: return None return prompts[-1] sessions=[] # each dict(start, end, list_texts, final) active=None def flush(final, end): if final is not None and final.strip(): sessions.append({'start':active['start'], 'end':end, 'texts':active['texts'], 'final':final}) for f in files: frame=int(os.path.basename(f)[1:6]) txt=open(f).read().splitlines() cur=last_prompt(txt) if cur is None: if active is not None: # flush active session final as last text if not already flush(active['final'], active['end']) active=None else: if active is None: active={'start':frame,'texts':[cur], 'final':cur} else: prev=active['final'] # Is cur a continuation (prefix extension) of prev or prev extension of cur? # Normalize by lower for check pl=prev.lower(); cl=cur.lower() cont = pl==cl or pl.startswith(cl) or cl.startswith(pl) or pl in cl or cl in pl if not cont: # new command started flush(active['final'], frame-3) active={'start':frame,'texts':[cur], 'final':cur} else: active['texts'].append(cur) # take longer text as final? If cur longer, final=cur; else keep prev (handle backspace) if len(cur)>len(active['final']): active['final']=cur # else keep if active is not None: flush(active['final'], 999999) print('total sessions',len(sessions)) for i,s in enumerate(sessions,1): print(i, s['start'], s['end'], repr(s['final']), '| variants:', ','.join(s['texts'][:10])) PY python3 /app/extract_sessions.py > /app/sessions_raw.txt 2>&1 head -100 /app/sessions_raw.txt)

[tool-a74] exec_command <- Fix and run session extractor ($ cat > /app/extract_sessions.py <<'PY' import glob, os, re files=sorted(glob.glob('/app/bottom_ocr/b*.txt')) def last_prompt(lines): prompts=[] for l in lines: s=l.strip() if s.startswith('>') and s != '>': txt=s[1:].strip() prompts.append(txt) if not prompts: return None return prompts[-1] sessions=[] active=None def flush(final, end): if final is not None and final.strip(): sessions.append({'start':active['start'], 'end':end, 'texts':active['texts'], 'final':final}) for f in files: frame=int(os.path.basename(f)[1:6]) txt=open(f).read().splitlines() cur=last_prompt(txt) if cur is None: if active is not None: flush(active['final'], active.get('end', active['start'])) active=None else: if active is None: active={'start':frame,'texts':[cur], 'final':cur, 'end':frame} else: active['end']=frame prev=active['final'] pl=prev.lower(); cl=cur.lower() cont = pl==cl or pl.startswith(cl) or cl.startswith(pl) or pl in cl or cl in pl if not cont: flush(active['final'], frame-3) active={'start':frame,'texts':[cur], 'final':cur, 'end':frame} else: active['texts'].append(cur) if len(cur)>len(active['final']): active['final']=cur if active is not None: flush(active['final'], active.get('end', active['start'])) print('total sessions',len(sessions)) for i,s in enumerate(sessions,1): print(i, s['start'], s['end'], repr(s['final']), '| variants:', ','.join(s['texts'][:15])) PY python3 /app/extract_sessions.py > /app/sessions_raw.txt 2>&1 wc -l /app/sessions_raw.txt head -70 /app/sessions_raw.txt)

The session extraction works but has issues: OCR noise creates spurious sessions (e.g., `mg,'`, `Dopen`, `»`), and also duplicate/merged sessions due to OCR line extraction weirdness. But the overall count is 646 sessions, far more than 262 moves because many commands are split into multiple sessions due to OCR variations and partial completion passage. We need to improve.

Notice many sessions of `n` (single char) because each `n` command is separate. Good, that reflects many moves. But there are also spurious due to OCR mistakes: e.g., `mg,` should be part of `move`; `»` etc.

Let's look at real sequence. The issue is that OCR bottom crop sometimes captures partial typing weirdly (like `>m` then `>mg,` etc.), and our continuation logic treats `mg,'` as new because it doesn't match `m` (cont check: pl='m', cl='mg,' => cl.startswith(pl) True, so it should continue; but session 28 shows 'mg,' as final because later `move` session starts at 462; hmm there are spurious).

We need a more robust approach. Let's print sessions with more context/frames for a segment to understand what's happening. But perhaps simpler: Instead of using bottommost prompt from bottom crop OCR, we can use the full-frame OCR (2fps) combined? No.

Another approach: Use OCR TSV for each frame to identify the bottommost prompt's y coordinate and text, with better fidelity. But we already have bottom OCR text.

Maybe the bottom crop at y=470-720 is too tall; it captures several command lines, and the last prompt line in OCR text may not be the current input if the OCR sorts lines incorrectly or because the screen has output below the prompt (e.g., after the command, output line may appear below the prompt and push prompt up; the last `>` line may still be old command while new output is below). The bottommost `>` line might not be the current input if there are non-prompt lines below? But the prompt command line is the last `>` line before the next output; after entering, output lines appear below it. For our session, that's okay because after pressing Enter, the command line stays (old) until future output pushes it, but no new prompt yet.

The spurious sessions may come from OCR misreading the prompt as different text between frames (e.g., `>open` vs `>e` vs `>open`--due to OCR reading partial frames of prior command?).

Let's try an improved automated reconstruction.

Observation: The bottom OCR at 10fps shows the typing in real-time. The correct set of commands is the set of complete commands, i.e., prompt lines that appear after user presses Enter. We can identify these by watching for the moment when the text after `>` stops growing and then the prompt becomes bare (or a new command starts). Our session approach captured many but with noise.

Let's improve:
1. Use only the bottommost `>` line as current input, but filter out lines that are too short/partial when a longer command follows.
2. Use OCR normalization and ignore spurious characters.

But the bigger issue is the session extraction splits/merges incorrectly because of OCR's partial detection of the bottommost line. Let's examine specific problem cases.

In frames 414/417, the prompt line is read as "Dope" / "Dopen_" but it's the same `>open`. Our session 22 `op_` at 411, session 23 `open` at 420, but there's a gap 414-417 where the bottommost `>` line wasn't detected as prompt because OCR read it as "Dope" (without `>`). So the session got split. The actual command was `open`. The bottom crop OCR is noisy: `>` gets misread as `D` sometimes (Dopen). We should normalize OCR errors: lines that start with `D` followed by known verbs like "open" are actually `>open`. Similarly `Dopen solid`, `Dopen case`, etc.

Also lines like `mw` at 423 (maybe `>w` misread). The bottom line may be read as `mw` instead of `>w`. Let's inspect~ Actually 423 shows "mw" maybe OCR of ">w" (the `>` misread as m). So many commands may be missed because OCR misreads `>` as other letters.

Here the prompt `>get all` is at crop y=136, and output below at y=162. So the prompt line can be above output? That is because after pressing Enter, the output is printed below the command line, so the prompt line is no longer at the bottom; output occupies below it. The current prompt line stays where it was typed, and output scrolls below it. The terminal is not line-oriented with fixed prompt at bottom; it's a scrolling transcript where each command line appears, and output appears after it. So the current input can appear anywhere in the viewport.

Thus isolating a fixed bottom line won't always capture the current prompt; it will capture output text below it. Hmm.

But the prompt line is always the last `>` line before the current output. The bottommost `>` line at any moment is the most recently entered command. This is what our session algorithm uses. It worked but OCR noise splits.

We can improve by using TSV coordinates to find the bottommost line that starts with `>` (at x≈128-130). Let's use TSV on bottom crops at 10fps (or maybe 5fps) to identify the bottommost prompt line, with better OCR confidence. But TSV is slower.

Actually, we can use the full-frame 2fps OCR text which already gives clean `>` lines. Perhaps let's use the full-frame prompt lists and reconstruct the move sequence manually/algorithmically with better OCR cleaning. Let's focus on that.

Let's take the full-frame (2fps) prompt list (the big output) and save to a file, then we can clean.

But there's a lot of partial frames. Perhaps we can use the bottom OCR 10fps to fill in exact typing and disambiguate.

Let's write a combined approach:

1. Use full-frame OCR2 prompt lines (clean enough) to build a raw chronological list of candidate commands, by taking, for each frame, all prompt lines but collapsing OCR variants.

Since full-frame OCR captures larger text windows, it sometimes shows multiple commands. We can detect new commands by observing the set of visible commands and the header Moves counter.

Better: Use the `Moves:` counter from full-frame OCR header to know when new moves happen蓬莱. We can parse the header from each full-frame OCR file (e.g., "Room Score: 15 Moves: 13"). There's usually a line at top containing "Score:" and "Moves:". Let's extract those. Then we know the move number at each frame. We can align commands typed between counter increments.

Let's extract header info from full OCR files.
[tool-cee] exec_command <- Extract Score/Moves headers from full OCR ($ python3 - <<'PY' import glob, os, re files=sorted(glob.glob('/app/ocr2/f*.txt')) prev=None for f in files: txt=open(f).read().splitlines() for i,l in enumerate(txt[:5]): m=re.search(r'Score:\s*(\d+)\s+Moves:\s*(\d+)', l) if m: print(os.path.basename(f)[:-4], 'score',m.group(1),'moves',m.group(2)) break PY | head -100)

The header extraction ran (the here-doc error at end due to earlier interactive weirdness, but output done). Some values are misread (e.g., f0047 moves 2), but generally we get a move counter timeline. We can use it to align commands.

Now, maybe we can reconstruct the complete move list by writing a script that, for each frame, records the prompt lines and uses the "Moves" counter to know which command corresponds to which move number.

Specifically:
- At each frame we see some visible prompt lines. The most recent command is the bottommost one. The header at that moment tells "Moves: N", meaning after N moves have been made. The bottommost prompt line corresponds to move N (the last move). If we know each frame's bottommost prompt and its "Moves" number, we can record that command as move N. This handles duplicates and order!

For example:
- f0022 moves=1, bottom prompt maybe `n` -> move 1 = n.
- f0023 moves=2, bottom prompt maybe `n` -> move 2 = n.
- etc.

This is promising. We need robust OCR of header Moves and bottommost prompt from the SAME full-frame OCR files (or from aligned bottom OCR). Full-frame OCR files may not always capture both header and bottom prompt if the prompt line is not in the file? But full-frame OCR gives entire screen, so it includes both header and the current bottom prompt. We can parse each full-frame OCR file:
- Extract Moves counter from the first line if present.
- Extract all prompt lines (they may be multiple). Identify the last prompt line in the OCR text (bottommost on screen). That likely corresponds to the latest move (or the command currently being typed). Since OCR text lines are ordered top-to-bottom, the last prompt line is bottommost.
- Record command text for that Moves count.

Potential issues:
- OCR may misread Moves occasionally (e.g., f0047 moves=2 due to OCR).
- During typing, the bottommost prompt may be partial (e.g., `>get e`, `>get eg_`). We should record only complete commands. But we can use the fact that a command is "entered" when the player presses Enter; the OCR at that moment shows the completed command and the Moves counter hasn't changed yet. The next frame after output shows updated Moves. Actually Moves counter increments when the command is processed (after Enter). At the frame where we see `>get bag` completed and the prompt is still present, the header may already show the new Moves count? Let's check: In f0049 header was "Moves: 29" and we saw `>get bag_` (partial) at f0049. Hmm.

Let's test alignment manually for a few:
- f0022 shows "Moves: 1" and prompt list has `>n`. So move 1 = n.
- f0023 shows "Moves: 2" and prompt `>n`. So move 2 = n.
- f0028 "Moves: 7" and prompts `>d | >s | >e | >op_`. The last prompt is `>op_` (partial) but moves=7. Actually the command `op_` is partial? Let's inspect f0028 and f0029.

The spurious sessions (28 `mg,`, 32 `op_`, etc.) arise because the OCR line being read as "bottommost prompt" alternates between the newly typed partial command and the previous command line still visible above. Our "last prompt line" is unreliable because after pressing Enter, the old command line stays and the new typed line may be read incorrectly (e.g., "Dope" instead of ">open case", so `last_prompt` picks `>move rug` from above, causing session split).

Thus the bottommost `>` line is not always the new command; sometimes the new command is typed but OCR fails to read its `>`, so it's ignored)Skip.

This suggests using the **full-frame OCR** which reads `>open case` correctly is better. The full-frame OCR at 2fps lists:
```
f0033 >w | >get all | >move rug | >op_
f0034 >w | >get all | >move rug
f0035 >w | >get all | >move rug
f0036 >w | >get all | >move rug | >open case | >open trap
f0037 >open case | >open trap | >d | >liL
```
Even though it sometimes reads `op_` partial, it identifies the new command `open case`, `open trap` when complete.

So let's use the full-frame OCR prompt lists to reconstruct. We need a more reliable way to identify command sequence from these lists.

Idea: Use the "Moves:" counter to segment commands. Consider the full-frame list:
- f0022 Moves:1 -> new command `n` (bottommost). Moves=1 means this is move 1.
- f0023 Moves:2 -> new command `n` (bottommost). Move 2 = n.
- f0024 no header; prompt list `>n | >n | >u`; bottommost `u` is new.
- f0025 `>n | >n | >u | >g` ; bottommost `g` new.
- f0026 `>get egg_` ; partial.
- f0027 `>d | >s` ; means `get egg` completed then `d`, then `s` shows.
- f0028 header Moves:7 and prompt list `>d | >s | >e | >op_`. So moves 3-7 are: get egg (3?), d (4?), s (5?), e (6?), open (7 partial...) Wait, the bottommost prompt is `op_`, and Moves=7. So `open` is move 7 (being typed?).
- f0029 `>s | >e | >open` -> `open` is move 7 completed, then `w` maybe move 8 (not shown in prompt list because OCR misread as `Dw`).
- f0030 `>open | >w | >get` -> `w` is move 8, `get` is move 9 (partial).

We can use the Moves counter to number commands. The bottommost **complete** prompt at each frame should correspond to the latest move count, once it's executed. But we need to catch every new command. If we record, for each frame, the bottommost **complete** prompt (last prompt line that doesn't end with `_` and isn't a known partial), we get the latest move. However as seen, sometimes the bottommost prompt is partial while full prompt is above (e.g., f0028 `>op_` while `open` full command appears at f0029). So we may record `op_` as move 7 prematurely; then next frame `open` appears as bottommost with same move count. We can resolve by continuing to update the latest move's command until it becomes complete.

Algorithm:
- Build a chronological list of frames, each with:
  - moves (if header present)
  - list of prompt texts (all `>` lines in OCR text, in top-to-bottom order).
- Maintain `last_move` = current move number being processed.
- For each frame:
  - Determine move count M from header if available.
  - Candidate commands: the prompt texts in frame.
  - The **latest command** is the last prompt text in the list (bottommost). But if it's a partial/scroll, we need better.
  - If M > last_move: a new move happened since last frame. The new command should be the bottommost prompt text (or the newest addition). We can set the command for move M.
  - If M == last_move but the bottommost prompt text changed from partial to full, update the command for current move.

Let's try implement using full-frame OCR at 2fps. But we need handle missing headers (many frames). We can infer move count from visible commands? Hmm.

Alternatively, use bottom OCR which has every 0.1s, and header from full OCR can be interpolated.

Let's output the full-frame prompt list to a file and then go through it sequentially, creating clean commands. We already have the list in the earlier output. Let's save it.
[tool-956] exec_command <- Save per-frame prompt list from full OCR ($ python3 - <<'PY' import glob, os, re files=sorted(glob.glob('/app/ocr2/f*.txt')) out=open('/app/full_prompt_list.txt','w') for f in files: txt=open(f).read().splitlines() prompts=[l.strip() for l in txt if re.match(r'^\s*>',l) and l.strip()!='>'] bare=sum(1 for l in txt if l.strip()=='>') if prompts or bare: out.write(os.path.basename(f)[:-4] + ': ' + ' | '.join(prompts) + (f' [bare]' if bare else '') + '\n') out.close() print(open('/app/full_prompt_list.txt').read()) PY)

This manual reconstruction is possible but lengthy. Maybe we should automate extraction with better logic leveraging the full-frame prompt list plus the **Moves counter** to assign commands to move numbers.

Let's write a script that:
- Parses full OCR files, extracting for each frame:
  - move counter if present
  - list of prompt texts
- Processes chronologically, maintaining a mapping from move number -> command.
- For each frame:
  - If a move counter M is present signal that we have processed up to M moves.
  - The "newest command" at this frame is the last prompt in list, but partial ones may be there.
- We want to determine the command text for each move number. A move completes when the player presses Enter. At the moment the complete command appears and the move counter increments, we can associate.

Maybe simpler: Use the fact that each move number N appears in one or more frame headers. At the earliest frame where Moves >= N, the command just typed is the **bottommost complete prompt** in that frame. But as seen, bottommost may be partial.

Let's implement with a "pending command" approach:
- Iterate frames. Maintain `expected_move` starting at 1.
- For each frame, examine the prompt lines.
- The **last prompt line** is the current input being typed (if any). If it's complete (no trailing `_` and not a prefix of a longer command that appears later), we can associate it with the current move being performed.
- Use move counter M to know how many moves have completed. When M == expected_move - 1, the current last prompt is the command for move `expected_move` (if complete). When typed command is partial, wait.

But we still have OCR noise.

[tool-ac9] exec_command <- Extract last prompt per frame from full_prompt_list ($ cd /app && python3 - <<'EOF' import re lines=[] for l in open('full_prompt_list.txt'): m=re.match(r'f(\d+):\s*(.*)',l) if not m: continue fr=int(m.group(1)); rest=m.group(2) # take last prompt text prompts=re.findall(r'>\s*([^|]*)',rest) prompts=[p.strip() for p in prompts if p.strip()] if prompts: lines.append((fr,prompts[-1])) for fr,p in lines: print(fr,repr(p)) EOF)

[tool-0eb] exec_command <- Find and zoom the actual prompt line ($ cd /app && python3 - <<'EOF' import cv2, numpy as np cap=cv2.VideoCapture('video.mp4') for fr in [672, 676]: cap.set(cv2.CAP_PROP_POS_FRAMES, fr) ret, frame=cap.read() # full frame: find last rows containing dark pixels gray=cv2.cvtColor(frame, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY) dark=(gray<128) rows=np.where(dark.any(axis=1))[0] if len(rows)==0: continue y0=rows.max()-40 crop=frame[max(0,y0):, :] from PIL import Image im=Image.fromarray(cv2.cvtColor(crop, cv2.COLOR_BGR2RGB)) im=im.resize((im.width*4, im.height*4), Image.LANCZOS) im.save(f'/tmp/p{fr}.png') print('ok') EOF for fr in 672 676; do echo "=== frame $fr ==="; tesseract /tmp/p${fr}.png stdout --psm 7 2>/dev/null; tesseract /tmp/p${fr}.png stdout --psm 6 2>/dev/null; done)
